Physically-modelled synthesiser · a Moog for the synths · General MIDI

GM Patch Coverage

All 128 programs, rated by how much reality has been allowed to contradict the model — from a shared base standing in, through a voice derived from how the instrument works, to one fitted against a recording of the thing itself.

The whole set at once

General MIDI’s program list is exactly sixteen families of eight, so the whole of it fits in one grid. Each cell is a program, coloured by its rating; a dashed outline marks a patch played by a voice of the wrong physical kind. The rest of the specification is not a list of sounds but a list of behaviours — how many voices, which channel is percussion, which controllers must respond — and that half is documented in midi.md rather than here.

Where it stands

9 119
  • 0nothingno voice at all
  • 1general classa shared base, or another instrument standing in
  • 2specific, theoryits own voice, derived from how the instrument works
  • 3specific, theory + ear...and validated semantically by ear, by whatever mechanism
  • 4specific, validated against a target...a recording of the instrument, or the hardware's own output

Nothing scores 0, because every program resolves to something — which is not the same as every program being served. The line that matters is the one at 4: audio of the instrument, analysed and fitted against. Published measurements that are not audio — the Rhodes’ high-speed-camera papers, the free reeds’ flow model — are better than theory, but they cannot contradict the model the way a recording can, so those voices sit at 2 and say why.

The class column is what the ROUTER returns, not the program map. Several programs are a family routed per note — the bowed and pizzicato ensembles, the brass section, the solo winds whose bottom octave is a different instrument — and reading the program map instead reports the no-note fallback. That is how GM 61 came to be listed as a trombone standing in for a whole section long after it had been routed to trumpet, trombone and tuba sections.

For a synth program the instrument is a synthesizer. The 32 synth programs — basses, strings, brass, leads, pads, effects and the synth drum — are patches on a Moog Messenger, and the Messenger engine is fitted against Ben’s own Messenger, recorded through a mixer and back (examples/messenger_fit.py): every knob law the patches use, from the cutoff and the contours to the waveshape, the FM index, LFO 1 and the output stage. Whole notes, attack to release, agree with the hardware within 0.6–2 dB on 28 of the 32. That is a recording of the instrument, so a patch that is the Messenger alone is a 4; one with a part no Messenger has — vocal formants after it, the guitars’ valve, the MF-104M pedal, which is modelled and not recorded — is a 3. And the reference runs both ways: a patch’s knobs are the hardware’s, so it plays on the Messenger itself.

Every one of the 128 programs now has a voice of its own kind; what remains is how much reality each has been allowed to contradict. None is played by a voice of the wrong physical kind: the last three were 123 Bird Tweet, 124 Telephone Ring and 125 Helicopter.

PatchClassNotes
0Acoustic Grand PianoGrandPiano4FITTED TO A STEINWAY B (VCSL, CC0; examples/upright_fit.py --ref=steinway) -- the model whose published law it had: inharmonicity by partial peaks (the real bass ~3x stiffer), spectrum and board, decay with its late tail, a 2 ms hammer, the strike's thump and noise (a pluck without them, Ben), unisons beating slowly and irregularly as the B's do (one ratio made a phaser). Levels held: VCSL's are normalized. The undamped strings ring sympathetically (the free-string register)
1Bright Acoustic PianoUprightPiano4an UPRIGHT, measured (VSCO-2 CE, Ivy Audio's): inharmonicity by partial peaks (short wound bass 4-9x the grand's, the scale break at C#3-F3), spectrum, decay with its late tail and register levels fitted per key (examples/upright_fit.py), a hammer contact shortening with force. A small board's brightness, which GM's name asks for; the grand voiced hard is kept as a voicing. Ben: very close, then great once the attack's knee went in
2Electric Grand PianoElectricGrand4a CP-70, FITTED TO PIANOBOOK'S "DAVID'S CP70" (Conner Sawchuk; 31 keys, 4-7 layers at their recorded levels, measured only; examples/upright_fit.py --ref=cp70): the short strings' 1/L^4 stiffness, derived, held (F1's B 12.6e-4 recorded, 12.0 derived); no soundboard, a piezo on the bridge; the decay fitted -- a fundamental that sings over fast upper partials (8.6 -> 6.8 dB); the hammers' brightness swinging far with velocity, dark soft and bright hard (13.4 -> 10.3 dB); the register's level (4.3 -> 3.5). The rest is one instrument's key-by-key variation. Ben: sounds great
3Honky-tonk PianoHonkyTonk3the measured upright (GM 1) with the tuner's hand off: unisons 8-20 cents, CC1 the wheel. The upright is fitted; the detune is judged from beat rates, not measured
4Electric Piano 1Rhodes4FITTED TO LEARMAN'S 1977 MARK I off the harp connector (jRhodes3d, CC BY-NC, measured only; examples/rhodes_fit.py), on what his EQ cancels -- the growl a harder layer adds, the hard attack's first 50 ms, the bark's fade, the decay (a tine sings 3-4x longer than we had it), the bass h1 BUILDING for seconds (F1 peaks 3.9 s in): the paper's own pickup (DAFx-17), a point tip over the magnet's frustum face on an arc, so a wide bass swing spikes each half cycle; each harmonic a sum of decaying layers, the swing's power series, at its true phase. Then voiced by ear: a forte that buzzes in the bass, mf untouched, and the buzz dying away faster than the tone (a wide swing damps faster); the register level even across the keyboard, as Gwazda's Wurlitzer measures (it had run 43 dB C2-C7 riding the swing). Ben, live: good and distinct from the Wurlitzer
5Electric Piano 2Wurlitzer4FITTED TO GWAZDA'S BALTIWURLI, a 200A (measured only; examples/wurli_fit.py), DI and speaker sets, the takes the recording's clipper spared: the reed swings through a SLOT in the plate (Pfeifle & Muenster, DAFx-17), a square-edged bump, not the one-sided 1/d first read (ladder h2-h32 14.6 -> 10.8 dB rms) -- centred it loses the fundamental; each harmonic's envelope a signed sum of exponentials, so a wide bass swing buzzes to h32 against the edges and its fundamental builds back as the swing decays (the lingering low attack, Ben); each layer at its true phase in the wave (in phase, a flat top came out lopsided); the reed's decay by register (log-rate 0.35); the action's thump from the Mech set, at the pack's own mix; the register level to the DI's own (6.2 -> 2.1 dB rms). Not modelled: a fixed ring near 387 Hz from E5 up. Ben: better on the low buzz; kept; live, good and distinct from the Rhodes
6HarpsichordHarpsichord4FITTED TO VCSL'S HARPSICHORDS (CC0; examples/harpsi_fit.py): the English Normal and Lute sets and the Flemish High and Low, each stop measured as its difference from the 8' on one instrument, so the room drops out. A STRING BANK, like the piano: every choir on a key is present, each slightly apart in cents, stiffness and partial tuning, and the stops (the CC11 word, rated below) choose which the jacks pluck; a key lifts every damper, so the undrawn choirs ring through the bridge. The quill plucks at fixed force, so the comb never fills and there are no dynamics. The ladder and body over the whole band, partials to 16 kHz, the decay by band (log-rate error 0.96 -> 0.65), random partial phases (in phase they swept like a phaser, Ben), and the quill's catch before it slips (3.5-6 ms, measured). Ben: sounds great
7ClavinetClavinet4FITTED TO A HOHNER D6 (Aalto's companion page to Gabrielli, Valimaki & Bilbao, ICMC 2011: Bb2, E4, F#5, each twice, measured only; examples/clav_fit.py) on the DAFx-12 pickup paper's geometry -- a 67.8 cm string at 161 Hz where the estimate had 0.26 m, the bridge pickup at 4 cm and the centre one 18.5-6.5 cm from the termination, the four switch settings (centre, bridge, both, anti-phase) -- the takes on the CENTRE pickup (6.7 dB rms, against 7.6 for the bridge); the ladder darkening up the keyboard (F#5's h2 25 dB down where the estimate had it level), a string that sings (Bb2 2-3 dB/s, not the estimate's 17), B 3.5e-5 measured (not 1.1e-4); and the KEY-UP, measured: a thump at the mechanism's fixed Hz plus the string let go at its own 2nd-4th harmonics, 35 dB under the strike and gone in ~30 ms -- live as well, stamped at the key-up. Three notes: the compass past them is the length law's. Ben: sounds good

52Choir AahsChoirAahs4FITTED TO VOCALSET'S LONG TONES (CC BY 4.0, measured only; examples/choir_fit.py): 20 conservatory singers, each by the voice type their readme names, every frame's harmonics -- the vibrato sweeps them across the tract. The old "ah" was 10 dB short at F2 and 20-30 dB short at 2.4-3.4 kHz, the singer's formant, and so it was against all four GM sets' choirs; weighted rms 11.1 -> 3.2 dB. A woman's whole cluster is weaker (not only the top formant), and a declared countertenor keeps a tenth of it (one singer: an estimate). The section from Dagstuhl ChoirSet's larynx microphones: each singer 14-22 cents off the note, vibrato +/-12 at 4.4-5.5 Hz. The air between the harmonics (51-77 dB under them, in the tract's shape; the room 15-25 dB under that), and EFFORT: +0.23 dB/octave brighter per dB louder, from 109 messa di voce notes. Not modelled: each singer's slow drift (8.5 cents rms under 1 Hz, independent between singers) and the harmonics' level swing under vibrato (under 1 dB at a choir's depth). Ben: sounds great, the wider section even better; the breath and effort, great

55Orchestra HitOrchestraHit4FITTED TO WHAT GM PLAYS (examples/orchhit_fit.py): the patch is the Fairlight-era SAMPLE, one recorded orchestral stab, so the GM sets' own hits are the instrument (FluidR3/MuseScore, GeneralUser GS, TimGM6mb; measured only). A root and its FIFTH (horns re-voiced onto it, winds onto the twelfth), flat across the octaves, a quarter of it noise, short (-20 dB at ~0.3 s; it rang 1.4 s) -- and the PERCUSSION: 55-74% of their power is under 400 Hz, the impact and then the chord's root three and four octaves down, so a TIMPANI on the root (our fitted kettle's own modes, a new pitched layer) and a cymbal above. A/B'd against FluidR3's own hit. Ben: sounds great now

104SitarSitar4Freesound sitars: a Ma string struck four times (CC0) and chinpen's D4 strokes (CC BY 3.0) (examples/pluck_fit.py): the decay law and its register slope on both; the gourd by absolute frequency, the jawari as the body falling away under a buzz that is there at once and rises quickly (a short banded late copy), each partial its own phase, a 1.5 ms onset -- all on the Ma take, chinpen's sitar having no growing buzz. Its sympathetic strings tuned on Sa; Indian intonation as a secondary tuning (ragas.py: just, Pythagorean, equal, Bel's Todi, 27 ragas measured from Saraga) and a meend that locks the wheel to the raga's swaras. Channel 10: the percussion note map

A separate specification from the 128 programs, and the place where the reference corpus divides most sharply. Iowa’s percussion pages carry cymbals, crotales and hand percussion — and no drum kit at all: no snare, no bass drum, no toms, which is 9,630 of that collection’s 12,781 percussion notes. DrumGizmo’s DRSKit (CC-BY 4.0), a recorded kit, fills the kick, the snare, the toms and the crashes hit hard; VSCO-2 CE and VCSL (CC0) the hand percussion, the claps, the whistles and the second snare; Freesound takes the timbales, the surdo (CC0) and the cuica (reinsamba, CC-BY). 111 of the 113 mapped notes are fitted against a recording.

Drum sets: the program on a drum channel

The program on a drum channel is the drum set, and a set changes only some notes — every blank in Roland’s table is “same as Standard”. The Sound Canvas’s sets on every map (SC-8850 owner’s manual p.187): the SC-55 map has 10 (and the CM-64/32L at 128), the SC-88 map 14, the SC-88Pro map 25, the SC-8850 map 37. A set not built here plays Standard. The goal: every one built. Each built set lists the notes it changes.

Where it stands: SC-8850 map 37 of 37, SC-88Pro map 25 of 25, SC-88 map 14 of 14, SC-55 map 10 of 10.

Stops: the registers within a program

A program’s rating is its default registration’s. A registerable program has other stops, and each is its own claim — the harmonium take plays two of its reeds, Piteå’s pipes measured three of the church organ’s fourteen stops — so each is rated on the same scale. The names are the code’s own (stop_ranks, checked when the page is built), the bit is the stop word’s, and the rows after the stops are the rest of the console: the Leslie’s speeds, the accordion’s banks. 69 of the 76 are fitted against a recording; where Piteå has a stop still to measure, the note names it.

6 Harpsichord

the harpsichord's registers (CC11 stop word)
BitStopNotes
084the lower 8': VCSL's English harpsichord over the whole band -- ladder, body, decay as m^0.6, partials to 16 kHz, the quill's catch before it slips -- and a string among three: the key's other choirs answer through the bridge (register.py), each its own cents and wire
18-upper4the Harpsimachord (Pianobook, a Kirckman copy), its Upper against its Lower: within +-3 dB at every harmonic, so plucked where the lower 8' is (0.115; the Flemish pair's ratio, 0.083, was 13.1 dB rms off, now 3.1) and dying 1.12x as fast (11 keys). Plucks ~3 ms after the lower 8' (asserted: no coupled recording)
244the Harpsimachord's Lower+4 against its Lower, the 4' read on the 8''s even harmonics against the odd ones beside them: plucked at 0.21 (twice the 8''s fraction), darker, dying faster (2.89 -> 1.62 dB rms); level 0.57 from the takes' totals (+0.9 dB), not the ladder's 1.05 -- Ben, A/B: "Levelled sounds more natural". Its own bridge (half the coupling, asserted)
3lute4the Harpsimachord's Buff against its Lower (VCSL's English Lute before it): a gentler buff, the treble 6-10 dB down at h3-h12 and the bass untouched (7.18 -> 2.20 dB rms), dying 1.62x as fast as the 8' (early partials); its tail is the other 8' answering, 5.7 cents off (VCSL's 5.8), through the register VCSL's tail fitted; the pad damps choir A's string whichever jack plucks it. Ben, A/B: "After lute sounds more natural"

23vox humana 84the Voix humaine 8 of the extended set's second organ (bit 23), the Grönlund having none: its own series at its anchor pitches, its speech and its level by Pitea's own balance, fitted to its pipes (examples/pitea_newranks.py). Ben, examples/organ_newstops_demo.py: they sound incredible
24trompette 84Pitea's swell Trompette harmonique 8 (bit 24): its own series at its anchor pitches, its speech and its level by Pitea's own balance, fitted to its pipes (examples/pitea_newranks.py). Ben, examples/organ_newstops_demo.py: they sound incredible
–pipe speech and release4fitted on the Principal 8 by register; every flue rank takes the law at its own pitch
–shared wind (the chest)3every pipe on the chest hears the others' demand: a chord dips the pressure as it enters and surges it as it lifts, a reservoir ringing at 4 Hz and a regulator restoring it -- the form the physics' (Pykett, Carlsson 2002, the Rodgers wind patent), the size Ben's ear's (flexibility 1 of off/1/2.5/8: a five-note chord takes ~2 cents from a held note). No recording of one organ's coupling was fitted: it varies organ to organ. On by default
–the key's groove2not modelled: the stops on a key share its channel, so a release drains faster with more drawn, and the pipes go flat as it drains (Pitea's reeds 35-68 cents at C3-C4)

20 Reed Organ

the harmonium's registers (CC43/44/45 stop word: bass | treble at E4/F4)
BitStopNotes
0cor anglais 14the bass 8' foundation: donyaquick's harmonium, 32 keys, the series within 2.5 dB rms

Variations: the GS banks

A GS bank select (CC0 = bank, then the program) picks a variation tone under a program (SC-55 owner’s manual p.68). 975 of GS’s 975 are built; one not built plays its capital tone, as a Sound Canvas does.

126.1LaughingSfxLaugh_sfx524the SFX set's key 52 played from the keyboard, C4 at its kit pitch (its gate, ring and level the kit's): LAUGHING: a "ho ho ho" (Ben's call; a woman's laugh never read as one) -- a throat pulsed as a Freesound Santa says it (CC0): four arcing "ho"s and a long falling "hooo", each glottal pulse ringing his /o/'s formants (LPC: 500, 850 Hz, 2.5, 2.9, 5.7 kHz; examples/throat_fit.py), so the pitch slides through a fixed vowel. 2.1 s where GeneralUser GS's laugh is 0.9
126.2ScreamingSfxScream_sfx534the SFX set's key 53 played from the keyboard, C4 at its kit pitch (its gate, ring and level the kit's): SCREAMING: a girl's scream (Freesound, CC0), its harmonics at her own levels (h2 -5 dB, h3 -12) over a breath 60 dB under, sliding up a fourth into 1.24 kHz as GeneralUser GS's does (weather_fit HARMCOL: colour 0.0 dB at the harmonics)
126.3PunchSfxPunch_sfx544the SFX set's key 54 played from the keyboard, C4 at its kit pitch (its gate, ring and level the kit's): PUNCH: a fist on a body (Freesound, CC0) as a 16 ms burst of shaped noise, its colour fitted (2.6 dB) -- as a body's modes it rang half a second
126.4Heart BeatSfxHeartbeat_sfx553the SFX set's key 55 played from the keyboard, C4 at its kit pitch (its gate, ring and level the kit's): HEART BEAT: not a recording -- the heart's own waveform, a textbook ECG beat's Fourier series, 1 Hz harmonics at the FFT's gains and phases (examples/ecg_heart.py: rendered, correlation 1.000) -- and over it the BLOOD FLOW as a Doppler hears it, noise filling to a top that follows an artery's velocity (3 kHz at peak; systole, the dicrotic notch, diastole's run-off). Ben: "sounds great"
126.5FootstepSfxFootstep_sfx564the SFX set's key 56 played from the keyboard, C4 at its kit pitch (its gate, ring and level the kit's): FOOTSTEPS1: a hard shoe on stone (Freesound, CC0): the heel as shaped noise (colour 1.0 dB) and, 70 ms on, the sole 8 dB under, as the recorded steps pair
126.6Applause 2OrchestraApplause_sfx584SC-88 map: set 56's key 58 from the keyboard
127.1MachinegunSfxMachinegun_sfx734the SFX set's key 73 played from the keyboard, C4 at its kit pitch (its gate, ring and level the kit's): MACHINEGUN: the set's gun shot (72) fired every 69 ms, 0.69 s of them, as a Freesound M16A1 burst measures (CC0) -- an impact train in the set's own stroke table (percussion_map.KIT_STROKES)
127.2LasergunSfxLaser_sfx743the SFX set's key 74 played from the keyboard, C4 at its kit pitch (its gate, ring and level the kit's): LASERGUN: a square wave swept down from 12x its note with a 0.1 s time constant, held and cut, as a Freesound blaster (CC0) measures. GeneralUser GS's is no reference (its envelope dies in 1 ms by the SF2; timidity plays a whine)
127.3ExplosionSfxExplosion_sfx754the SFX set's key 75 played from the keyboard, C4 at its kit pitch (its gate, ring and level the kit's): EXPLOSION: a dynamite blast (Freesound, CC0; weather_fit: 0.4 dB), at once, held 0.6 s, dying over the next second as GeneralUser GS's does

Sound Canvas: the GS maps

Each Sound Canvas’s map of tones — the SC-55’s own (owner’s manual p.68), and the SC-55mkII, SC-88 and SC-88Pro maps from the SC-88Pro manual’s instrument list — against ours: a tone is built when we play it, as a program or a variation, and rated as the tables above rate it. A later map can put another tone under a number we built (the SC-88’s 4.8 is “St.Soft EP”, not our detuned pair); that one is not counted.

Effects: the Sound Canvas’s

Rated on the voices’ scale: 4 validated against a target (a recording of the device, or the hardware’s output of a file judged against ours by ear), 3 validated semantically by ear, 2 from theory (Roland’s tables, our models), 1 a stand-in, 0 not built. The reverb is the module’s way of giving the music a space, and our physical rooms are how we do that job. Each insertion type is to be mapped onto the physical model we have where one exists — Rotary the Leslie, Overdrive and Distortion the valve amp and cabinet, the choruses chorus.py, the 3D types our room and head.

EffectNotes
Reverb (system)3the macro picks one of our physical rooms by what it is for, small to large as Roland's run: Room 1-3 studio, chamber, a larger chamber; Hall 1 our concert hall, Hall 2 a grand one (Musikverein-scale); Plate an EMT 140 plate and Delay / Panning Delay a 100 ms echo, on the studio's walls (roomtail.device_ir) -- heard in examples/gs_reverb_demo.py (Ben: those all sound good). Its level and time scale the tail; CC91 a distance. Tokei was validated in the chamber under Hall 1 (now our hall: Ben chose the studio for it). Character, Pre-LPF, pre-delay and a per-drum reverb send not yet
Controller matrix (GS 40 2x, GM2 09 0s)3ctrlmatrix.py: GS's six sources (mod, bend, both aftertouches, the assignable CC1/CC2) and GM2's (channel and poly pressure, any CC) into amplitude (the fader's rows; an organ's gates), pitch (the bend row), cutoff (brightness at onset), LFO pitch depth (a sine on the bend row, so a held note's aftertouch vibrato moves), LFO amplitude depth (sidebands on rows of their own) and LFO rate -- measured in examples/ctrlmatrix_demo.py (Ben: phase 1 sounds good). The mod wheel's own vibrato (GS's 47-cent default) held out of files (TUNING_MOD_VIBRATO), LFO filter depth and live not yet
Chorus (system)3chorus.gs_copies: Roland's eight macros, a line swept by one LFO, read at two outputs in opposite phase, fed back; laws GM 2's (rate, depth, feedback), the delay's chosen (0.2 ms a step); the wet share held as CC93's. To reverb / to delay, per-drum chorus sends, not yet
Delay (system)3gsdelay.py: Roland's three taps, time table, ten macros, feedback through the center; CC94 (in a GS file) and the drum keys' sends; level laws chosen (linear over 127). Delay to reverb not yet a send of its own
EQ (system)2the SC-88's two shelves at 200/400 Hz and 3/6 kHz, per part on/off, first-order (the order chosen)

Insertion effects (EFX, the SC-88Pro’s 64 types)

Read per note at its onset (the files automate the level, the mix, the sends); a part assigned to a type not built plays dry.

TypeNotes
Stereo-EQ3two shelves at their own corners and two peaking mids (gsefx.eq_gain), every partial; heard on sysex_01 (Ben: "These all sound great")
Spectrum3eight fixed peaking bands at the manual's frequencies, each partial's gain at its own; heard on a piano
Enhancer3efxbus: what lies above 2 kHz saturated, its harmonics mixed back by Mix; heard on an EP
Humanizer3efxfilter: three formants of its vowel (a/i/u/e/o), gliding at its Accel; its drive the valve's gain stage; heard on a Moog lead
Overdrive3the OD a gain stage in front of the part's valve (tubeamp, its gains multiplying), its output makeup, the 1x12 cabinet; low/high gains after it. Amp Type (Small/BltIn/2-/3-Stack) one speaker for all; heard in the OD -> Chorus/Flanger/Delay demos (Ben, 2026-10-21: those sound great)
Distortion3as Overdrive, with more gain; heard on storm_pro (kathie, SC-88Pro), its part 8 clean electric guitar at Drive 70 (Ben: sounds good)
Phaser3efxfilter: four all-passes at the swept corner, its resonance and mix, on every partial (the lobe kernel's filter_grid); heard on our riff
Auto Wah3efxfilter: a resonant LPF/BPF swept up (or down) from its Manual by its LFO, and opened by its Sens (the part's notes' envelope); heard on our riff and a Clav (Ben: "sounds great")
Rotary3our Leslie at the type's rotor speeds, switched by Speed, Accl through our measured spin-ups, the rotors' levels -- RECORDED as Roland's is: two mics round the cabinet (Separate, 0-180 degrees), each to its speaker with its own swing and Doppler; heard on our Rhodes riff, mono and then with its two mics (Ben: "Those sound awesome", "Sounds great")
Stereo Flanger3the chorus device with a flanger's swing, Roland's pre-delay and feedback, its outputs apart by its Phase; heard on our riff (Ben: "sound great")
Step Flanger3efxfilter: the flanger's comb, its line swept in steps; heard on our riff
Tremolo3the tremolo pass, Roland's five waves as Fourier series, the depth dipping the level; heard on our riff
Auto Pan3the same across the ears; heard on our riff
Compressor3efxbus: a level-following gain at the output's scale, 10-40 dB down at 8:1, attack 1-50 ms (chosen); heard on our guitar riff
Limiter3efxbus: the compressor at its Threshold and Ratio, Release 10 ms-1 s (chosen); heard on our guitar riff
Hexa Chorus3six voices spread in delay, depth and pan by its Dev parameters (0.5 ms a Pre Dly Dev step, chosen); heard in its demo (Ben, 2026-10-21: these all sound great)
Tremolo Chorus3the chorus and the tremolo pass (half depth, chosen); heard in its demo (Ben, 2026-10-21: these all sound great)
Stereo Chorus3as Space D, its Phase and pre-filter (the LPF; its HPF not built); heard in its demo (Ben, 2026-10-21: these all sound great)
Space D3chorus.gs_copies, two outputs half a turn apart, pre-delay and rate from Roland's tables, Balance the share; heard on SENSATIO
3D Chorus3the chorus's outputs at 60 degrees, past the speakers; heard in its demo (Ben, 2026-10-21: these all sound great)
Stereo Delay3gsdelay: left and right taps each looping at its own period, feedback (cross for ping-pong), phase inversion, HF damping; heard on our riff (Ben: "They sound great")
Mod Delay3the Stereo Delay with its modulation as the repeats' vibrato; heard on our riff
3 Tap Delay3centre, left and right taps, the centre's loop; heard on our riff
4 Tap Delay3four taps spread left to right (chosen); heard on our riff
Tm Ctrl Delay3one tap at its time, placed by its pan (its time's glide under control not built); heard on our riff
Reverb3efxbus: a decaying noise response per ear, its highs dying to HF Damp, six types by density, tail and early reflections (chosen); heard on an EP
Gate Reverb3efxbus: a noise burst Gate Time long convolved with the part -- Norm, Reverse, the two Sweeps; heard on Melodic Tom

Fb P.Shifter3a shifted tap whose repeats shift again round the loop; heard on our riff (Ben: "sound great")
3D Auto3the same, turning at its Speed; each note where its onset finds it; heard on our riff (Ben: "sound great")

Lo-Fi 13efxbus, the part's own bus: held to a lower rate, quantised to fewer bits (Type 1-9, chosen), its pre and post low-passes; heard on an EP and sysex_01 (Ben: "sounds great")
Lo-Fi 23efxbus: its rate and bits, a filter after, and noises heard through silence -- a radio's detune and static, white/pink, a record's crackle (33/45/78 rpm), mains hum; heard on an EP (Ben: "sounds great")

Roland GS SysEx: the address map

What the file renderer reads of Roland’s GS map (the VE-GS Pro MIDI Implementation; the SC-88Pro manual for the insertion effects). Yes read and applied; Partial read, with its limit; No not read. Live reads the part parameters, the rhythm part, the master settings and the reset, on port A.

51 rows: 24 read, 8 partly, 19 not.

The class tree

Every one of the 427 voice classes a program or a drum note is routed to, hung under its physical base — what the instrument is, struck or plucked or blown — with any mixin that adds a behaviour beside its name. After each, the programs (GM n), percussion notes and drum-set notes that play it, coloured by their rating. A branch with nothing beside it is a shared base no patch plays directly.
